1) File: rendering.py — edit `choose_scale_mode()` (top of file). Replace the body with:

```python
def choose_scale_mode(raw_min, raw_max, eps=1e-10):
    """
    Heuristic to pick a display scale mode.
    Returns one of: 'normalize', 'sqrt', 'log', 'raw'
    """
    if raw_max <= 0:
        return 'normalize'
    ratio = raw_max / max(raw_min, eps)

    # Be conservative: prefer normalize unless dynamic range is large
    if raw_max > 10000 or ratio > 100:
        return 'log'
    if ratio > 20:
        return 'sqrt'
    return 'normalize'
```

2) File: rendering.py — inside `build_spectrum_array(...)` replace the transform/normalize block (the region where you compute `eps` and handle `'log'/'sqrt'/'raw'/'normalize'`) with this exact code (keeps percentile clipping to avoid over-amplifying background):

```python
    eps = 1e-10

    # Clip extreme outliers to the 99.5th percentile BEFORE applying log/sqrt.
    # This prevents tiny background from being amplified by the transform.
    if spectrum.size:
        p_high = float(np.percentile(spectrum, 99.5))
    else:
        p_high = raw_max
    clipped = np.minimum(spectrum, p_high)

    if scale_mode == 'log':
        s = np.log1p(clipped)
        spectrum_normalized = s / (s.max() + eps)
    elif scale_mode == 'sqrt':
        s = np.sqrt(clipped)
        spectrum_normalized = s / (s.max() + eps)
    elif scale_mode == 'raw':
        spectrum_normalized = spectrum / (raw_max + eps)
    else:
        spectrum_normalized = spectrum / (raw_max + eps)

    # Slightly enhance mid/high values for crisper peaks
    spectrum_normalized = np.power(spectrum_normalized, 1.4)
```

Why this helps (one line each)
- Higher thresholds in `choose_scale_mode` make auto pick normalize unless the dynamic range is truly large.
- Percentile clipping prevents log/sqrt from amplifying faint baseline noise caused by many small glow contributions.
- Slightly lower power (1.4) keeps peaks crisp without exaggerating background.

If you want, I can apply these two exact edits as a patch. Which do you prefer?

User: just keep normalise. what's the difference between normalise and raw?

GitHub Copilot: Short answer: use Normalize — it linearly scales intensities to 0–1 and is what you want.

Details (brief)
- Normalize: divides the built spectrum by its global maximum so values are 0 → 1 (linear scaling). This preserves relative peak heights and renders as you expect.
- Raw: in your current code it does the same as Normalize (it also divides by the global max for rendering stability), so there is no practical difference right now.
- True “raw” (no normalization) would keep absolute intensities, but that usually breaks display (values can be huge or tiny) so the renderer normalizes it anyway.

```python
        # Improved normalize:
```

GitHub Copilot: Yes — that makes sense. To show exactly 7 visible lines, pick the top 7 peaks in the summed spectrum and zero everything else (preserving a small neighborhood around each peak). Paste this into rendering.py inside `build_spectrum_array(...)` right after the loop that builds spectrum (i.e., immediately after the line where the loop ends and before you compute `raw_max`).

Exact code to insert:

```python
# -- keep only the top N peaks (preserve small neighborhood) --
def _keep_top_n_peaks(spectrum, wl_array, n=7, min_sep_nm=0.4):
    if n <= 0 or spectrum.size == 0:
        return spectrum
    # candidate count (a few times n to allow clustering)
    k = min(spectrum.size, max(n * 5, n))
    cand_idx = np.argpartition(spectrum, -k)[-k:]
    # sort candidates by height desc
    cand_idx = cand_idx[np.argsort(spectrum[cand_idx])[::-1]]
    selected = []
    # convert separation in nm to index distance
    dx = wl_array[1] - wl_array[0]
    sep_idx = max(1, int(round(min_sep_nm / dx)))
    for i in cand_idx:
        if all(abs(i - s) > sep_idx for s in selected):
            selected.append(int(i))
            if len(selected) >= n:
                break
    # build mask preserving small neighborhood around selected peaks
    mask = np.zeros_like(spectrum, dtype=bool)
    for s in selected:
        lo = max(0, s - sep_idx)
        hi = min(spectrum.size, s + sep_idx + 1)
        mask[lo:hi] = True
    new_spec = np.zeros_like(spectrum)
    new_spec[mask] = spectrum[mask]
    return new_spec

# call it with N=7 (adjust min_sep_nm if needed)
spectrum = _keep_top_n_peaks(spectrum, wl_array, n=7, min_sep_nm=0.4)
# -------------------------------------------------------------
```

Notes:
- Put this immediately after your per-line summation loop and before computing `raw_max`/normalization.
- `min_sep_nm` prevents selecting nearby samples as separate peaks; set smaller (e.g., 0.2) if peaks are very close, larger if they are spaced.
- This preserves the relative shape around each peak; normalization afterwards will scale them for display.
- If you later want the number (7) configurable from the UI, I can show how to pass it via `spectral_data` or the web form.

Want the exact insertion point with a 1–2 line context from your file so you can paste confidently?

  ```
  # If requested, keep only the top `keep_top_n` visible peaks (preserve a small neighborhood)
  if keep_top_n and keep_top_n > 0:
      s = spectrum.copy()
      # convert minimum separation in nm to index distance
      dw = wl_array[1] - wl_array[0]
      min_sep_idx = max(1, int(round(min_sep_nm / dw)))
      # indices sorted by descending intensity
      idx_desc = np.argsort(s)[::-1]
      accepted = []
      for idx in idx_desc:
          if s[idx] <= 0:
              break
          if all(abs(idx - a) > min_sep_idx for a in accepted):
              accepted.append(idx)
              if len(accepted) >= keep_top_n:
                  break
      # build mask preserving a neighborhood around each accepted peak
      mask = np.zeros_like(s, dtype=bool)
      for a in accepted:
          lo = max(0, a - min_sep_idx)
          hi = min(len(s), a + min_sep_idx + 1)
          mask[lo:hi] = True
      spectrum = s * mask.astype(float)
  ```

Searched for text `# Preserve raw stats for diagnostics and flexible scaling` (`Graphing` · `spectrum/rendering.py`), 1 result

Replace lines 111–134 in rendering.py with the following robust top‑N filter (keeps up to N peaks even if separation rules would exclude some):

Lines to replace: rendering.py

Replacement code:
```python
    # If requested, keep only the top `keep_top_n` visible peaks (preserve a small neighborhood)
    if keep_top_n and keep_top_n > 0:
        s = spectrum.copy()
        # convert minimum separation in nm to index distance
        dw = wl_array[1] - wl_array[0]
        min_sep_idx = max(1, int(round(min_sep_nm / dw)))
        # indices sorted by descending intensity (only positive)
        idx_desc = np.argsort(s)[::-1]
        idx_desc = idx_desc[s[idx_desc] > 0]
        accepted = []
        # First pass: honor minimum separation
        for idx in idx_desc:
            if all(abs(idx - a) > min_sep_idx for a in accepted):
                accepted.append(int(idx))
                if len(accepted) >= keep_top_n:
                    break
        # If separation prevented collecting enough peaks, relax and fill with next-highest indices
        if len(accepted) < keep_top_n:
            for idx in idx_desc:
                if int(idx) not in accepted:
                    accepted.append(int(idx))
                    if len(accepted) >= keep_top_n:
                        break
        # Build mask preserving a neighborhood around each accepted peak
        mask = np.zeros_like(s, dtype=bool)
        for a in accepted:
            lo = max(0, a - min_sep_idx)
            hi = min(len(s), a + min_sep_idx + 1)
            mask[lo:hi] = True
        spectrum = s * mask.astype(float)
```

Notes:
- This guarantees up to `keep_top_n` peaks are preserved; if fewer valid (positive) peaks exist, it preserves them all.
- If you want stricter behavior (never relax separation) remove the second fill loop.

User: do i have to manually set the "keep top N peaks" ?

GitHub Copilot: No — you don't have to set it manually. You can auto-detect N from the spectrum. Two robust options (pick one):

1) Peak-detection (recommended, precise)
- Use scipy.signal.find_peaks with a prominence/height and minimum separation to find visible peaks, set N = number of accepted peaks.
- Fallback: if scipy not available, use a percentile cutoff on the raw spectrum.

2) Threshold/percentile (simple)
- Choose all local values above a percentile of positive intensities (e.g. 99th or 95th) and use that count as N.

Example to insert into `render_spectrum` immediately after the first raw build (replace after your first call that produces `wl_array, spectrum_normalized, ..., raw_stats`):

```python
# derive raw (unnormalized) spectrum for peak finding
raw_s = spectrum_normalized * float(raw_stats.get('raw_max', 1.0))

# if keep_top_n not provided or 0, auto-determine it
if not keep_top_n:
    try:
        from scipy.signal import find_peaks, peak_prominences
        # min separation ~0.2 nm -> convert to index
        dw = wl_array[1] - wl_array[0]
        min_sep_idx = max(1, int(round(0.2 / dw)))
        # find peaks with small height threshold (2% of max)
        peaks, _ = find_peaks(raw_s, height=raw_stats.get('raw_max', 0.0)*0.02, distance=min_sep_idx)
        if peaks.size:
            prominences = peak_prominences(raw_s, peaks)[0]
            # keep peaks with prominence >= 40th percentile (tunable)
            thr = np.percentile(prominences, 40)
            peaks = peaks[prominences >= thr]
        auto_n = int(peaks.size)
    except Exception:
        # fallback: count values above the 99.5th percentile of positive intensities
        pos = raw_s[raw_s > 0]
        if pos.size:
            thresh = np.percentile(pos, 99.5)
            auto_n = int((raw_s >= thresh).sum())
        else:
            auto_n = 0

    # clamp to at least 0
    keep_top_n = max(0, auto_n)
    spectral_data['diagnostics']['auto_keep_top_n'] = keep_top_n
```

Notes:
- Tune `0.2 nm`, `0.02` (height fraction), and percentile/prominence thresholds to match your visual expectations.
- If you want absolutely deterministic behavior without SciPy, use the fallback percentile approach.
- After this, your existing flow (apply top-N only for normalize) will use the computed `keep_top_n`.

Want a ready apply_patch snippet that inserts this exact block into rendering.py at the right spot?

User: i told you i wanted it to be auto-detected. that was literally in my initial request. 

GitHub Copilot: Read [](file:///c%3A/Users/viran/Downloads/indep.%20project/Graphing/spectrum/rendering.py#208-208), lines 208 to 236

Read [](file:///c%3A/Users/viran/Downloads/indep.%20project/Graphing/spectrum/rendering.py#236-236), lines 236 to 268

Replace lines 219–235 in rendering.py with the block below to auto-detect `keep_top_n` when the user leaves it 0:

File: rendering.py

Replacement (paste verbatim):
```python
    keep_top_n = int(spectral_data.get('keep_top_n', 0) or 0)
    # first build (unfiltered) to get raw stats for auto-pick
    wl_array, spectrum_normalized, component_counts, raw_stats = build_spectrum_array(
        spectral_data['wavelengths'],
        spectral_data['intensities'],
        spectral_data['width_factors'],
        spectral_data['actions'],
        scale_mode='raw',
        keep_top_n=0
    )

    # Auto-detect keep_top_n when the user left it as 0 (auto mode)
    if keep_top_n == 0:
        # reconstruct raw (unnormalized) spectrum from returned normalized + raw_max
        raw_max = float(raw_stats.get('raw_max', 0.0) or 0.0)
        raw_s = spectrum_normalized * (raw_max if raw_max > 0 else 1.0)

        # try scipy peak detection first (more robust); fallback to percentile method
        auto_n = 0
        try:
            from scipy.signal import find_peaks, peak_prominences
            dw = wl_array[1] - wl_array[0]
            min_sep_idx = max(1, int(round(0.2 / dw)))  # ~0.2 nm min separation
            height_thr = raw_max * 0.02 if raw_max > 0 else 0.0
            peaks, props = find_peaks(raw_s, height=height_thr, distance=min_sep_idx)
            if peaks.size:
                prominences = peak_prominences(raw_s, peaks)[0]
                prom_thr = np.percentile(prominences, 40) if prominences.size else 0.0
                peaks = peaks[prominences >= prom_thr]
            auto_n = int(peaks.size)
        except Exception:
            pos = raw_s[raw_s > 0]
            if pos.size:
                thr = np.percentile(pos, 99.5)
                auto_n = int((raw_s >= thr).sum())
            else:
                auto_n = 0

        keep_top_n = max(0, auto_n)
        diagnostics = spectral_data.get('diagnostics', {})
        diagnostics['auto_keep_top_n'] = keep_top_n
        spectral_data['diagnostics'] = diagnostics

    # Auto-select scale if requested
    if scale_mode in (None, 'auto'):
        chosen_mode = choose_scale_mode(raw_stats.get('raw_min', 0.0), raw_stats.get('raw_max', 0.0))
    else:
        chosen_mode = scale_mode
```

- `spectral_data['diagnostics']['auto_keep_top_n']` records the chosen N.
- Later code (unchanged) applies top‑N only when `chosen_mode == 'normalize'`.

GitHub Copilot: Compacted conversationI’m sorry — let me fix this for you. Paste the replacement block below into rendering.py, replacing the existing auto-detect section (lines 226–273). Also set the keep-top-N input default to 0 in your template.

Replacement code (paste whole block over that range):

```python
    keep_top_n = int(spectral_data.get('keep_top_n', 0) or 0)
    # first build (unfiltered) to get raw stats for auto-pick
    wl_array, spectrum_normalized, component_counts, raw_stats = build_spectrum_array(
        spectral_data['wavelengths'],
        spectral_data['intensities'],
        spectral_data['width_factors'],
        spectral_data['actions'],
        scale_mode='raw',
        keep_top_n=0
    )

    # Auto-detect keep_top_n when the user left it as 0 (auto mode)
    if keep_top_n == 0:
        raw_max = float(raw_stats.get('raw_max', 0.0) or 0.0)
        raw_s = spectrum_normalized * (raw_max if raw_max > 0 else 1.0)

        auto_n = 0
        # Prefer SciPy if available (more robust); otherwise use a numpy-only fallback
        try:
            from scipy.signal import find_peaks, peak_prominences
            dw = wl_array[1] - wl_array[0]
            min_sep_idx = max(1, int(round(0.2 / dw)))  # ~0.2 nm min separation
            height_thr = raw_max * 0.02 if raw_max > 0 else 0.0
            peaks, props = find_peaks(raw_s, height=height_thr, distance=min_sep_idx)
            if peaks.size:
                prominences = peak_prominences(raw_s, peaks)[0]
                if prominences.size:
                    prom_thr = np.percentile(prominences, 25)
                    peaks = peaks[prominences >= prom_thr]
            auto_n = int(peaks.size)
        except Exception:
            s = raw_s
            if s.size >= 3:
                # simple local-maxima detector
                candidates = np.where((s[1:-1] > s[:-2]) & (s[1:-1] > s[2:]))[0] + 1
                if candidates.size:
                    # height threshold: use stronger of percentile or small fraction of max
                    pos = s[s > 0]
                    height_thr = raw_max * 0.02 if raw_max > 0 else 0.0
                    perc_thr = np.percentile(pos, 95) if pos.size else 0.0
                    thr = max(height_thr, perc_thr)
                    peaks = candidates[s[candidates] >= thr]
                    # enforce minimum separation, picking strongest first
                    if peaks.size:
                        dw = wl_array[1] - wl_array[0]
                        min_sep_idx = max(1, int(round(0.2 / dw)))
                        peaks_sorted = peaks[np.argsort(s[peaks])[::-1]]
                        accepted = []
                        for idx in peaks_sorted:
                            if all(abs(int(idx) - a) > min_sep_idx for a in accepted):
                                accepted.append(int(idx))
                        auto_n = len(accepted)
                else:
                    auto_n = int((s > (raw_max * 0.02)).sum())
            else:
                auto_n = int((s > 0).sum())

        keep_top_n = max(0, int(auto_n))
        diagnostics = spectral_data.get('diagnostics', {})
        diagnostics['auto_keep_top_n'] = keep_top_n
        spectral_data['diagnostics'] = diagnostics
```


Exact visual meaning of the three sensitivity profiles (so you know what you’ll get):

- Conservative  
  - Visual effect: only the very strongest, isolated emission lines remain. Small shoulders, low‑prominence bumps, and closely adjacent smaller peaks are suppressed.  
  - When to expect: dense noise or many tiny shoulders; output becomes sparse and emphatic (2–10 lines typical for moderate data).  
  - Result in Normalize: very few bright narrow columns on the RGB strip; broad background largely removed.

- Medium (default)  
  - Visual effect: picks clear peaks a human would identify as distinct lines, and will keep resolved nearby doublets if they show distinct local maxima. Small shoulders are usually excluded.  
  - When to expect: typical instrumental spectra where real lines stand above a smooth background.  
  - Result in Normalize: a human‑matching set of peaks (your provided example would yield about 7); background and low bumps suppressed but resolved close peaks are preserved.

- Aggressive  
  - Visual effect: includes lower‑intensity local maxima and many small peaks/shoulders, including tight clusters; less background suppression.  
  - When to expect: when you want to preserve as many candidate lines as possible for later inspection.  
  - Result in Normalize: many bright columns, possibly dozens or hundreds if the data contains many small local maxima.

Behavior notes you should expect
- `min separation = 0`: detector can accept peaks that are adjacent in wavelength (no enforced nm gap); practical peak selection still requires local maxima and the prominence/height threshold, so it won’t just pick every sample.  
- SciPy present: detection will be slightly more robust at separating true peaks from noise. SciPy absent: NumPy fallback still detects local maxima and uses percentile/height rules.  
- If auto detects 0 peaks, the Normalize view will fall back to showing the full spectrum (no hidden lines).  
- Diagnostics returned with each render: **auto_keep_top_n**, **spectrum_raw_min**, **spectrum_raw_max**, **spectrum_scale_mode**.  
- Output: both dark and light PNGs as requested.

User: keep in mind that multiplets exist in other spectra. these need to still be accurately depicted

GitHub Copilot: Short answer: I understand — multiplets (true closely‑spaced multiple lines) must remain separate. Right now the renderer clusters nearby detections with a fixed 0.5 nm gap, which can merge some real multiplets. 

What’s happening now (brief)
- Detection finds local maxima (SciPy or NumPy fallback).  
- We then group peaks closer than `group_gap_nm = 0.5 nm` and keep only the single maximum per group. That removes small shoulders/background but can merge multiplets that you want to show.

Options to preserve multiplets
1. Lower the grouping gap to match expected multiplet separations (recommended quick fix) — e.g. 0.10–0.2 nm. This preserves multiplets separated by that amount.  
2. Use prominence/valley‑depth clustering (better): only merge two peaks when the valley between them is shallow enough (i.e., the local minimum between them is a high fraction of the peak heights). This preserves multiplets even if they’re very close, as long as they form distinct local maxima separated by a deep enough notch.  
3. Adaptive hybrid: use a small grouping gap (e.g. 0.12 nm) AND require low valley depth to merge — conservative but robust.

My recommendation
- Implement option 2 (prominence/valley‑depth clustering). It best distinguishes true multiplets from shoulders and avoids an arbitrary nm cutoff. I’ll fall back to option 1 (small gap ~0.12 nm) if you prefer a simpler change.

Pick one and I’ll implement it:
- “prominence-cluster” — implement valley-depth merging (recommended).  
- “small-gap 0.12” — set group gap to 0.12 nm only.  
- “leave” — keep current behavior.

Which do you want?

User: fine. option 2

Completed with input: {
  "workspaceRoot": "c:\\Users\\viran\\Downloads\\indep. project\\Graphing",
  "codeSnippet": "import numpy as np\nfrom spectrum.loader import load_spectral_data\nfrom spectrum.rendering import build_spectrum_array\n\nfile_path = 'he test.xlsx'\nwith open(file_path, 'rb') as f:\n    file_bytes = f.read()\n\nspectral = load_spectral_data(file_bytes, sheet_name=None, user_title='he test')\n# first build raw\nwl_array, spectrum_normalized, component_counts, raw_stats = build_spectrum_array(\n    spectral['wavelengths'], spectral['intensities'], spectral['width_factors'], spectral['actions'], scale_mode='raw', keep_top_n=0\n)\nraw_max = float(raw_stats.get('raw_max', 0.0) or 0.0)\nraw_s = spectrum_normalized * (raw_max if raw_max > 0 else 1.0)\n\n# detection logic from rendering.py\npeaks = np.array([], dtype=int)\ntry:\n    from scipy.signal import find_peaks, peak_prominences\n    dw = wl_array[1] - wl_array[0]\n    min_sep_nm = 0.0\n    min_sep_idx = int(round(min_sep_nm / dw)) if dw > 0 else 0\n    height_thr = raw_max * 0.02 if raw_max > 0 else 0.0\n    peaks, props = find_peaks(raw_s, height=height_thr, distance=min_sep_idx)\n    if peaks.size:\n        prominences = peak_prominences(raw_s, peaks)[0]\n        if prominences.size:\n            prom_thr = np.percentile(prominences, 40)\n            peaks = peaks[prominences >= prom_thr]\n        if peaks.size:\n            group_gap_nm = 1.0\n            valley_rel_thresh = 0.5\n            dw = wl_array[1] - wl_array[0]\n            group_gap_idx = max(1, int(round(group_gap_nm / dw))) if dw > 0 else 1\n            peaks_sorted = np.sort(peaks)\n            clusters = []\n            cur = [peaks_sorted[0]]\n            for p in peaks_sorted[1:]:\n                if p - cur[-1] > group_gap_idx:\n                    clusters.append(cur)\n                    cur = [p]\n                    continue\n                lo = int(cur[-1])\n                hi = int(p)\n                if hi <= lo:\n                    cur.append(p)\n                    continue\n                valley = float(np.min(raw_s[lo:hi+1]))\n                last_peak_val = float(raw_s[cur[-1]])\n                this_peak_val = float(raw_s[p])\n                smaller = min(last_peak_val, this_peak_val)\n                if smaller > 0 and (valley / smaller) >= valley_rel_thresh:\n                    cur.append(p)\n                else:\n                    clusters.append(cur)\n                    cur = [p]\n            clusters.append(cur)\n            chosen = []\n            for cl in clusters:\n                vals = raw_s[np.array(cl)]\n                chosen_idx = cl[int(np.argmax(vals))]\n                chosen.append(int(chosen_idx))\n            peaks = np.array(chosen, dtype=int)\nexcept Exception:\n    s = raw_s\n    peaks = np.array([], dtype=int)\n    if s.size >= 3:\n        candidates = np.where((s[1:-1] > s[:-2]) & (s[1:-1] > s[2:]))[0] + 1\n        if candidates.size:\n            pos = s[s > 0]\n            height_thr = raw_max * 0.02 if raw_max > 0 else 0.0\n            perc_thr = np.percentile(pos, 95) if pos.size else 0.0\n            thr = max(height_thr, perc_thr)\n            peaks = candidates[s[candidates] >= thr]\n            if peaks.size:\n                group_gap_nm = 1.0\n                valley_rel_thresh = 0.5\n                dw = wl_array[1] - User: how are you currently finding the peaks? because i had google colab find my peaks and it did it perfectly

GitHub Copilot: Plan — I'll (1) list the exact detection steps and thresholds used, (2) point to the code, and (3) note why your raw input intensities can differ from the detected intensities and what I already did to preserve exact input values.

How peaks are found (exact pipeline and thresholds)
- Build raw continuous spectrum: `build_spectrum_array()` creates a continuous array from your input lines using Gaussian cores + a small glow halo (per-line sigma multipliers and actions). See rendering.py for the implementation.
- Raw reconstruction for detection: the code first calls `build_spectrum_array(..., keep_top_n=0, scale_mode='raw')` and reconstructs `raw_s = spectrum_normalized * raw_max`.
- SciPy branch (preferred):
  - Detector: `scipy.signal.find_peaks`.
  - Height threshold: `height >= raw_max * 0.02` (2% of the raw continuous spectrum max).
  - Distance: min separation set to 0 nm (converted to index, effectively no enforced distance).
  - Prominence filter: compute peak prominences and keep only those with prominence >= 40th percentile of the peak prominences.
  - Clustering + multiplet preservation:
    - Group gap: peaks within `group_gap_nm = 1.0 nm` are considered for clustering.
    - Valley-depth test: compute the valley (minimum) between adjacent peaks; merge peaks only if valley >= 50% of the smaller peak height (valley_rel_thresh = 0.5). This rule preserves true multiplets (keeps close peaks separated if there is a deep notch).
    - For clusters that are merged, the single strongest index is chosen as the cluster representative.
- NumPy fallback:
  - Local maxima detection (s[1:-1] > neighbors).
  - Threshold: max(2% of raw_max, the 95th percentile of positive values).
  - Same clustering + valley-depth logic as above.
- Final keep-top behavior:
  - `auto_keep_top_n = detected_count` is set from the detection above.
  - The top-N filtering (masking) is applied only if the chosen display `scale_mode == 'normalize'`. The masking in `build_spectrum_array()` preserves a neighborhood around each picked column (neighborhood width uses the `min_sep_nm` parameter; the function default is 0.4 nm unless overridden).
- Where to look: detection logic is in the `render_spectrum()` function in rendering.py (the SciPy/NumPy branches and clustering are implemented there).

Why intensities differed and what I did
- The "intensity" returned by the peak finder is the amplitude in the continuous reconstructed spectrum (`raw_s`) at the picked wavelength column — i.e., the continuous-spectrum peak height after Gaussian spreading, glow, and background-thresholding — not necessarily the original per-line intensity you input.
- To address your requirement for exact original intensities, I mapped each detected peak back to the nearest input line and exported the exact input intensities in detected_peaks_enriched.json / `.csv`. That file contains both the detected continuous-spectrum intensity and the original input intensity for each detected peak.

If you want the exact Colab behavior
- Tell me the exact Colab parameters (or share the Colab code snippet). I can:
  - replicate its `find_peaks` parameters exactly, or
  - adapt our thresholds (height / prominence / clustering) to match Colab and re-run detection and rendering.
Which would you prefer?

# --- Dynamic Prominence Calculation ---
# Calculate the range of 'Grey Val' in the filtered data
min_grey_val = df_filtered['Grey Val'].min()
max_grey_val = df_filtered['Grey Val'].max()
grey_val_range = max_grey_val - min_grey_val

# Define a prominence as a percentage of the overall range
# You will likely need to adjust this percentage (e.g., 0.05 for 5%, 0.1 for 10%)
# A higher percentage will be more selective, finding fewer, stronger peaks.
prominence_percentage = 0.12 # Increased to 0.12 to filter more aggressively
dynamic_prominence = prominence_percentage * grey_val_range

  "codeSnippet": "import numpy as np, json\nfrom spectrum.loader import load_spectral_data\nfrom spectrum.rendering import build_spectrum_array\nfrom scipy.signal import find_peaks\n\nwith open('he test.xlsx','rb') as f:\n    file_bytes = f.read()\n\nspectral = load_spectral_data(file_bytes, sheet_name=None, user_title='he test')\nwl_array, spectrum_normalized, component_counts, raw_stats = build_spectrum_array(\n    spectral['wavelengths'], spectral['intensities'], spectral['width_factors'], spectral['actions'], scale_mode='raw', keep_top_n=0\n)\nraw_max = float(raw_stats.get('raw_max',0.0) or 0.0)\nraw_s = spectrum_normalized * (raw_max if raw_max>0 else 1.0)\n\ndef cluster_peaks(peaks, raw_s, wl_array, group_gap_nm=1.0, valley_rel_thresh=0.5):\n    if len(peaks)==0:\n        return []\n    dw = wl_array[1]-wl_array[0]\n    group_gap_idx = max(1, int(round(group_gap_nm/dw))) if dw>0 else 1\n    peaks_sorted = np.sort(peaks)\n    clusters=[]\n    cur=[peaks_sorted[0]]\n    for p in peaks_sorted[1:]:\n        if p-cur[-1]>group_gap_idx:\n            clusters.append(cur); Short answer: different detection parameters and grouping. With the current pipeline (prominence = 12% of the spectrum range, essentially no enforced nm separation, and the continuous-spectrum shape we build), there are many small local maxima — that yields 254 clustered peaks.

What I checked (quick diagnostics):
- I scanned prominence percentage from 1%–50% and found 254 clustered peaks at 12% (the value you asked for). See prom_scan.json (summary).
- Increasing prominence or enforcing a minimum wavelength separation reduces counts. Example results (from the diagnostic run):
  - prominence 0.12 → clustered = 254
  - raising prominence or using a min separation reduces peaks; combos that produce exactly 7 include:
    - prom_pct=0.85 (85%) with min_sep_nm=0 → 7 peaks
    - prom_pct=0.7 with min_sep_nm=2 nm → 7 peaks
    - prom_pct=0.3 with min_sep_nm=20 nm → 7 peaks
- I saved detailed diagnostics to prom_diagnostic.json and outputs/prom_match_search.json.

Why this happens (concise):
- The continuous spectrum (Gaussians + glow) produces many small shoulders and local maxima; a low prominence (12%) plus fine sampling yields many detected peaks.
- Colab's run that found 7 peaks likely used different parameters (much higher effective prominence, an explicit min separation, a percentile-based prominence filter, smoothing, or a top-N filter). Any of those would cut the count down to ~7.

If you want me to reproduce the Colab result exactly, I can:

import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import seaborn as sns
import numpy as np
from scipy.signal import find_peaks

# Filter the DataFrame for 'nm' values between 400 and 750
df_filtered = df[(df['nm'] >= 400) & (df['nm'] <= 750)].copy()

# Sort by 'nm' to ensure correct neighbor comparison and plotting order
df_filtered = df_filtered.sort_values(by='nm').reset_index(drop=True)

plt.figure(figsize=(12, 7))
sns.lineplot(data=df_filtered, x='nm', y='Grey Val', marker=None) # No markers
plt.title('Grey Val vs. nm (nm range 400-750) with Sharp Peak Labels')
plt.xlabel('nm')
plt.ylabel('Grey Val')
plt.grid(True)

# Set x-axis limits and reverse the order
plt.xlim(750, 400) # Set limits from 750 to 400, automatically reverses

# --- Dynamic Prominence Calculation ---
# Calculate the range of 'Grey Val' in the filtered data
min_grey_val = df_filtered['Grey Val'].min()
max_grey_val = df_filtered['Grey Val'].max()
grey_val_range = max_grey_val - min_grey_val

# Define a prominence as a percentage of the overall range
# You will likely need to adjust this percentage (e.g., 0.05 for 5%, 0.1 for 10%)
# A higher percentage will be more selective, finding fewer, stronger peaks.
prominence_percentage = 0.12 # Increased to 0.12 to filter more aggressively
dynamic_prominence = prominence_percentage * grey_val_range

print(f"Calculated dynamic prominence: {dynamic_prominence:.2f}")
# Use scipy.signal.find_peaks to identify sharp peaks
# 'prominence' parameter helps in filtering stronger peaks (adjust as needed)
# A higher prominence value will select fewer, stronger peaks.
peaks, _ = find_peaks(df_filtered['Grey Val'], prominence=dynamic_prominence) # Using dynamic prominence

# Label the identified sharp peaks
for peak_index in peaks:
    row = df_filtered.iloc[peak_index]
    plt.annotate(f"{row['nm']:.2f} nm", # Formatted nm to two decimal places
                 (row['nm'], row['Grey Val']),
                 textcoords="offset points", # Offset the text
                 xytext=(0,10), # Distance from point to label
                 ha='center', # Horizontal alignment
                 bbox=dict(boxstyle="round,pad=0.3", fc="red", ec="b", lw=0.5, alpha=0.7),
                 arrowprops=dict(facecolor='black', shrink=0.05))

plt.show()

import numpy as np
from spectrum.loader import load_spectral_data
sd = load_spectral_data('he test.xlsx')
input_wls = np.array(sd['wavelengths'])
input_vals = np.array(sd['intensities'])
mask = (input_wls >= 400) & (input_wls <= 750)
input_wls_f = input_wls[mask]
input_vals_f = input_vals[mask]
min_g = float(np.min(input_vals_f))
max_g = float(np.max(input_vals_f))
rng = max_g - min_g
prom_pct = 0.08
dyn = prom_pct * rng
print('rng', rng, 'dyn', dyn)
from scipy.signal import find_peaks
peaks,props = find_peaks(input_vals_f, prominence=dyn)
print('peaks found',peaks)

import numpy as np
from spectrum.loader import load_spectral_data
from scipy.signal import find_peaks
sd = load_spectral_data('he test.xlsx')
input_wls = np.array(sd['wavelengths'])
input_vals = np.array(sd['intensities'])
mask = (input_wls >= 400) & (input_wls <= 750)
input_vals_f = input_vals[mask]
min_g = float(np.min(input_vals_f))
max_g = float(np.max(input_vals_f))
rng = max_g - min_g
print('min,max,rng:', min_g, max_g, rng)
for pct in [0.01,0.02,0.04,0.06,0.08,0.10,0.12,0.15,0.2]:
    dyn = pct * rng
    peaks, props = find_peaks(input_vals_f, prominence=dyn)
    print(f'pct={pct:.3f} dyn={dyn:.3f} -> {len(peaks)} peaks')
# Also try normalization variant (0-1)
norm_vals = (input_vals_f - min_g) / (rng if rng!=0 else 1)
for pct in [0.01,0.02,0.04,0.06,0.08,0.10,0.12]:
    dyn2 = pct * 1.0
    peaks2, _ = find_peaks(norm_vals, prominence=dyn2)
    print(f'normalized pct={pct:.3f} dyn={dyn2:.3f} -> {len(peaks2)} peaks')

import numpy as np
from spectrum.loader import load_spectral_data
from scipy.signal import find_peaks
sd = load_spectral_data('he test.xlsx')
input_wls = np.array(sd['wavelengths'])
input_vals = np.array(sd['intensities'])
mask = (input_wls >= 400) & (input_wls <= 750)
input_wls_f = input_wls[mask]
input_vals_f = input_vals[mask]
order = np.argsort(input_wls_f)
input_wls_f = input_wls_f[order]
input_vals_f = input_vals_f[order]

Read [](file:///c%3A/Users/viran/Downloads/indep.%20project/Graphing/outputs/he_test_colab_peaks.csv)

Short answer: the difference came from data ordering and preprocessing.

Why it differed
- Colab sorts the DataFrame by wavelength before peak detection (`df_filtered.sort_values(by='nm')`). find_peaks expects the signal indexed by increasing wavelength so neighbors correspond to nearby wavelengths. My earlier code ran detection on the loader output without sorting, so the sequence was in the sheet's original order (which in your file was not wavelength-ordered), producing very different (or zero) peak detections.
- Other possible but secondary causes: differences in which column is used (Colab used the raw `Grey Val` column), any earlier filtering/normalization in Colab, and library/environment differences (SciPy versions affect behavior slightly). But the primary issue was the ordering.

What I changed and validated
- I fixed `render_spectrum()` to sort the discrete input arrays by wavelength before calling `find_peaks`, matching the Colab preprocessing.
- I ran a diagnostic sweep and a direct test; with the Colab-style dynamic prominence (0.12 × range) the discrete signal now yields 7 peaks for `he test.xlsx` — the same set I saved to he_test_colab_peaks.csv.
- I regenerated annotated images: he_test_dark_colab.png and he_test_light_colab.png.

If you want next
- I can run the same Colab-style detection on `Oxygen NIST.xlsx` and save annotated outputs.

Good — I preserved multiplets. Quick summary and choices:

- What I did
  - Colab-style detection now runs on the discrete, wavelength-sorted rows and finds peaks using prominence = 0.12×range.
  - By default the heatmap is hidden and only the primary vlines are shown (so lesser surrounding glow/shoulders are removed).

  - Outputs written: he_test_dark_colab.png, he_test_light_colab.png, and he_test_colab_peaks.csv.

- Where to configure (in `spectral_data` or before calling `render_spectrum()`):
  - `colab_prominence` (float) — override prominence fraction (default 0.12).
  - `colab_keep_width_nm` (float) — >0 to preserve small neighborhood around each peak (nm); default 0 = keep exact index only.
  - `colab_multiplet_gap_nm` (float) — grouping threshold to detect multiplets (default 0.5 nm).
  - `colab_show_heatmap` (bool) — True to show masked heatmap; default False (only vlines).

Done — the shine is now peak-relative.

How it maps now (exact)
- rel = intensity / max_peak_intensity (linear)
- final 2D profile per peak = vertical_profile * (rel * horizontal_component)
- composite normalized to [0,1] before coloring

GitHub Copilot: **Center Line Code**

- **File:** rendering.py

```python
    # Draw Colab-style vertical peak lines (do not mutate original intensities)
    min_brightness = float(spectral_data.get('colab_min_brightness', 0.1))
    # linewidth range when scaled by peak intensity
    min_lw = float(spectral_data.get('colab_min_linewidth', 1.0))
    max_lw = float(spectral_data.get('colab_max_linewidth', 6.0))
    # If diagnostics requested suppression (we've rendered synthetic glows), skip vlines
    suppress_vlines = diagnostics.get('suppress_colab_vlines', False)
    if not suppress_vlines:
        for p in colab_peaks:
            try:
                base_rgb = wavelength_to_rgb(p['wl'])
                # prefer peak-relative normalization if available
                rel = float(p.get('peak_norm', p.get('norm', 1.0)))
                final_intensity_scale = min_brightness + (1.0 - min_brightness) * rel
                colored_rgb = (base_rgb[0] * final_intensity_scale,
                               base_rgb[1] * final_intensity_scale,
                               base_rgb[2] * final_intensity_scale)
                # scale linewidth so brighter peaks draw thicker lines
                lw = float(min_lw + (max_lw - min_lw) * rel)
                ax.vlines(x=p['wl'], ymin=0, ymax=1, color=colored_rgb, linewidth=lw)
            except Exception:
                continue
```

User: first, show me the code that's doing the glow.

GitHub Copilot: Here are the glow-related sections in rendering.py — file: rendering.py

- In `build_spectrum_array()` (per-peak core + glow added to the 1D spectrum):
```python
# Build core (Gaussian)
core = intensity * np.exp(-0.5 * ((wl_array - wl) / sigma) ** 2)

# Build glow halo
glow_sigma = sigma * 1.8
glow = (intensity * GLOW_STRENGTH) * np.exp(-0.5 * ((wl_array - wl) / glow_sigma) ** 2)

# Add components
spectrum += core + glow
```

- In the Colab-style synthetic peak construction (per-peak horizontal component `horiz`):
```python
gauss = np.exp(-0.5 * ((wl_array - center) / sigma) ** 2)
glow_sigma = sigma * 2.2
glow = np.exp(-0.5 * ((wl_array - center) / glow_sigma) ** 2)
horiz = gauss + (glow_strength * 0.35) * glow
```

- In the vertical profile (how the 2D glow/core are combined into columns):
```python
core_profile = v_core_amp * np.exp(-0.5 * ((y - 0.5) / v_core_sigma) ** 2)
glow_profile = v_glow_amp * np.exp(-0.5 * ((y - 0.5) / v_glow_sigma) ** 2)
base_profile = core_profile + glow_profile

# tip cuts to produce slim-top / fat-middle / slim-bottom silhouette
top_tip = tip_cut_amp * np.exp(-0.5 * ((y - 1.0) / tip_cut_sigma) ** 2)
bot_tip = tip_cut_amp * np.exp(-0.5 * ((y - 0.0) / tip_cut_sigma) ** 2)
vertical_profile = base_profile - (top_tip + bot_tip)
```

If you want sharper glow (less diffuse) I can:
- reduce the glow strength (e.g., lower `colab_glow_strength` / `colab_glow_reduce`), or
- decrease `v_glow_sigma` or `glow_sigma` multipliers, or
- boost `v_core_amp` / `colab_core_boost` for a crisper core.
